<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove the second Kahn–Kalai conjecture. For every finite simple graph <i>H</i> with <i>h</i> ≥ 1 edges and at most <i>n</i> vertices, the threshold for $`G(n,p)`$ to contain an ordinary copy of <i>H</i> is at most $`C p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)(1+\log_2 h)`$, where <i>C</i> is universal. Here $`p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)`$ is the least density at which every subgraph of <i>H</i> has expected copy count at least one half.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every $n\ge2$ and every finite simple graph $H$ with $h=e(H)\ge1$ edges and at most $n$ vertices, $$\label{eq:stronger} p_{\mathrm c}(n,H)\le \min\{1,\;2048\mathrm e^{50}p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)(1+\log_2 h)\}.$$ In particular, $$p_{\mathrm c}(n,H)\le6144\mathrm e^{50}p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)\log_2 n.$$
  - Explanation: Bounds the ordinary-copy containment threshold by the graph expectation threshold times a logarithmic edge-count factor; also gives a host-vertex formulation.
- **C2** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-local)): Suppose that $\|A_b\|\le m$ for a positive integer $m$, the law $\nu$ is $a$-spread, and $$0<\rho<1,\qquad a/\rho\le\mathrm e^{-50}.$$ Let $W\sim\mu_\rho$, independently of the source choice, and perform the transition above. Call the transition a *local failure* if $$Z(Y)<\mathrm e^{-10m}\quad\text{or}\quad \|T\|>m/2.$$ Then $$\label{eq:local-failure} \mathbb P(\text{local failure})\le\mathrm e^{-9m}.$$ Moreover, for each fixed target $b$ and every nonnegative function $f$ depending only on $W\cap(X\setminus A_b)$, $$\label{eq:distortion} \mathbb E\!\left[ \mathbf 1_{\{\mathrm{target}=b,\ Z(Y)\ge\mathrm e^{-10m}\}} f(W) \right] \le \mathrm e^{11m}\nu_b\,\mathbb Ef(W).$$ The expectation on the right is with respect to $W\sim\mu_\rho$.
  - Explanation: Controls the failure probability of local posterior resampling and its change of measure on coordinates outside the target label.
- **C3** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-tree)): Let $\mathcal T$ be a disjoint probability tree on a finite set $X$, with $k\ge1$ levels. Suppose that every level is $\sigma$-spread, where $\sigma>0$, and that its positive integer capacities satisfy $$\ell_{i+1}\ge16\ell_i\qquad(1\le i<k).$$ Set $$r=\min\{1,\;16\mathrm e^{50}\sigma(1+\log_2\ell_k)\}.$$ Then $$\mathbb P_{W\sim\mu_r} \bigl(\mathcal U(P)\subseteq W\text{ for some path }P\text{ of }\mathcal T\bigr) \ge\tfrac23.$$
  - Explanation: Covers a complete path union in a disjoint probability tree with geometrically separated capacities using a product sample.
- **C4** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-reduction)): Let $\mathcal T$ be a disjoint probability tree with $k\ge1$ levels and positive integer capacities $m_1,\ldots,m_k$ satisfying $m_{j+1}\ge16m_j$. Suppose level $j$ is $\alpha_j$-spread, where $$0<\alpha_j\le a,\qquad \rho=\mathrm e^{50}a<1.$$ There is a deterministic procedure which, given $W\sim\mu_\rho$, succeeds with probability at least $3/4$ and on success returns a disjoint probability tree $\mathcal T'$ with the same levels, such that:* 1. *level $j$ has capacity $\lfloor m_j/2\rfloor$ and is $\alpha_j/(1-\mathrm e^{-m_j})$-spread;* 2. *for every path $Q$ of $\mathcal T'$ there is a path $P$ of $\mathcal T$ with $$\label{eq:recovery} \mathcal U(P)\subseteq W\cup\mathcal U(Q).$$
  - Explanation: Deterministically prunes and reweights the input tree from one product sample, halving capacities and retaining a recovery guarantee.
- **C5** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-hierarchy)): There is a disjoint probability tree on $X=\binom{[n]}2$ whose path unions are copies of $H$ with its isolated vertices removed, such that every level is $\sigma$-spread and its label sizes $\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_k$ satisfy $$1\le\ell_1,\qquad \ell_{i+1}\ge16\ell_i, \qquad \ell_k\le e(H).$$
  - Explanation: Constructs graph-copy extension histories with disjoint labels, geometrically separated sizes and spread conditional child laws.

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Ordinary graph copies** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For finite simple graphs $G$ and $F$, write $N(G,F)$ for the number of subgraphs of $G$ isomorphic to $F$.
- **Binomial random graph** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Let $G(n,p)$ be the random graph on $[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}$ in which each edge is present independently with probability $p$.
- **Containment and graph expectation thresholds** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For a graph $H$ with at least one edge and at most $n$ vertices, define $$\begin{aligned} p_{\mathrm c}(n,H) &=\inf\{p\in[0,1]:\mathbb P(N(G(n,p),H)>0)\ge\tfrac12\},\\ p_{\mathrm E}(n,H) &=\inf\{p\in[0,1]:\mathbb EN(G(n,p),F)\ge\tfrac12 \text{ for every subgraph }F\subseteq H\}. \end{aligned}$$
- **Product random subset** ([source](#pm-h-4)): For a finite set $X$ and $0\le\rho\le1$, let $\mu_\rho$ be the law of a random subset of $X$ that includes each element independently with probability $\rho$.
- **Spread probability law** ([source](#sec:resampling)): For $a>0$, a probability distribution on subsets $A\subseteq X$ is *$a$-spread* if $$\label{eq:spread} \mathbb P(J\subseteq A)\le a^{\|J\|} \qquad(\varnothing\ne J\subseteq X).$$
- **Probability tree** ([source](#sec:trees)): A *probability tree on $X$* is a finite rooted tree with all leaves at the same depth, together with a probability distribution $\nu_v$ on the children $\operatorname{Ch}(v)$ of each non-leaf node $v$ and a label $A_v(b)\subseteq X$ on the arc from $v$ to each child $b$.
- **Disjoint tree** ([source](#sec:trees)): The tree is *disjoint* if the labels along each path are pairwise disjoint.
- **Level capacity** ([source](#sec:trees)): A level has *capacity* $m$ if all its labels have size at most $m$.
- **Level spread** ([source](#sec:trees)): A level is *$a$-spread* if the child distribution at every node immediately above that level is $a$-spread.
- **Ordinary copy counts** ([source](#eq:copy-ratio)): For a graph $F$ without isolated vertices, let $$M(F)=N(K_n,F),\qquad M(I,F)=N(I,F).$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-lem-local) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-local): Bayes density identity, spread intersection bound and independence outside target labels. -> Local failure and outside-target distortion bounds.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-reduction): Backward pruning using a recursively defined potential and conditioning on good target children. -> Reduced tree, spread preservation and recovery inclusion.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-thm-tree) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-reduction-2): Iterate reductions with fresh product samples, bound spread inflation by an infinite product and waiting time by Markov inequality. -> A capped logarithmic-density product sample covers a full path union.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-lem-hierarchy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy): Choose ratio-maximizing predecessors, retain distinct copy histories, and apply maximality to conditional edge probabilities. -> Disjoint graph-copy hierarchy with spread extension labels.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy-2): Apply tree covering to the graph hierarchy and compare edge count with the square of host vertex count. -> Both main containment threshold upper bounds.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I1**: Ordinary-copy counts count distinct edge sets, allowing additional host edges; vertex embeddings are not copy counts.
- **I2**: For a uniformly chosen ordinary copy of I, containment of a fixed copy F0 of F has probability M(I,F)/M(F).
- **I3**: The empty edge set has both copy-count normalizations equal to one.
- **I4**: The main containment bound concerns a finite simple target with at least one edge in a binomial independent-edge host.
- **I5**: The tree covering argument requires labels to be disjoint along each full path.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A1**: Use ordinary-copy counts for induced motif detection. -> Extra edges invalidate induced motifs but are allowed here.
- **A2**: Convert a dependency or correlated network into a binomial host without justification. -> The probability model and hence the threshold guarantee can fail.
- **A3**: Treat vertex embeddings as distinct copies. -> Automorphisms multiply counts and change the expectation threshold.
- **A4**: Reuse the tree reduction when path labels overlap. -> Descendant potentials need not depend only on outside coordinates.
- **A5**: Apply the graph theorem to directed, weighted or multiple-edge targets. -> Such targets fall outside the stated finite-simple-graph setting.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. Finite constructive subroutine for the stated ordinary-copy symmetry identity at eq:copy-ratio. It enumerates exact copy counts and fixed-copy containment probabilities on bounded hosts. It does not implement or certify the general threshold theorem, spreadness, hierarchy selection, posterior resampling or tree reduction.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON list of {n,I,F}; I,F are lists of unordered vertex pairs.
# PRECONDITIONS: 2 <= n <= 4; nonempty simple I; F subset I; vertices are integers in [0,n).
# OUTPUTS: JSON list of ordinary-copy counts and exact containment probability numerator/denominator.
# TIME: O(B*n!*n^2) for B cases; n is bounded above by 4.
# SPACE: O(B*2^(n*(n-1)/2)*n^2), including cached distinct edge-set copies.
# ARITHMETIC: Python exact integers and kit python-flint fmpq; no floating-point operations.
# FAILURE: invalid input raises ValueError and exits nonzero; no threshold certification is attempted.
import itertools, json, sys
from functools import lru_cache
from flint import fmpq

def edges(raw, n):
    if not isinstance(raw, list):
        raise ValueError('edge list required')
    result = []
    for e in raw:
        if not isinstance(e, list) or len(e) != 2 or any(type(v) is not int for v in e):
            raise ValueError('integer pair required')
        u, v = sorted(e)
        if not 0 <= u < v < n:
            raise ValueError('invalid simple edge')
        result.append((u, v))
    if len(set(result)) != len(result):
        raise ValueError('duplicate edge')
    return frozenset(result)

@lru_cache(None)
def copies(n, pattern):
    vertices = sorted({v for e in pattern for v in e})
    images = []
    for placement in itertools.permutations(range(n), len(vertices)):
        mapping = dict(zip(vertices, placement))
        images.append(frozenset(tuple(sorted((mapping[u], mapping[v]))) for u, v in pattern))
    return set(images)

def solve(case):
    n = case['n']
    if type(n) is not int or not 2 <= n <= 4:
        raise ValueError('n outside finite contract')
    I, F = edges(case['I'], n), edges(case['F'], n)
    if not I or not F <= I:
        raise ValueError('nonempty I and F subset I required')
    CI, CF = copies(n, I), copies(n, F)
    contained = sum(F <= image for image in CI)
    inside = sum(image <= I for image in CF)
    probability = fmpq(contained, len(CI))
    return {'M_F': len(CF), 'M_I_F': inside, 'M_I': len(CI),
            'containing': contained, 'p_num': int(probability.numerator),
            'p_den': int(probability.denominator)}

data = json.load(sys.stdin)
if not isinstance(data, list) or not 1 <= len(data) <= 1000:
    raise ValueError('bounded nonempty batch required')
print(json.dumps([solve(c) for c in data], sort_keys=True))
```

    "oracle_code": "# Independent idiom: enumerate host edge subsets and use graph isomorphism, never vertex embeddings.\nimport json as js\nimport sys as system\nfrom itertools import combinations\nfrom functools import cache\nfrom fractions import Fraction\nimport networkx as nx\n\n@cache\ndef iso_class(order, edge_tuple):\n    target = nx.Graph()\n    target.add_edges_from(edge_tuple)\n    universe = tuple(combinations(range(order), 2))\n    answer = []\n    for selection in combinations(universe, len(edge_tuple)):\n        graph = nx.Graph()\n        graph.add_edges_from(selection)\n        if nx.is_isomorphic(graph, target):\n            answer.append(frozenset(selection))\n    return tuple(answer)\n\ncases = js.loads(open(system.argv[1]).read())\nactual = js.loads(open(system.argv[2]).read())\nif system.argv[3] != 'count=%d' % len(cases) or not isinstance(actual, list) or len(actual) != len(cases):\n    system.exit(4)\nfor instance, observed in zip(cases, actual):\n    host = frozenset(tuple(sorted(pair)) for pair in instance['I'])\n    fixed = frozenset(tuple(sorted(pair)) for pair in instance['F'])\n    host_orbit = iso_class(instance['n'], tuple(sorted(host)))\n    small_orbit = iso_class(instance['n'], tuple(sorted(fixed)))\n    hit = len([s for s in host_orbit if fixed.issubset(s)])\n    inner = len([s for s in small_orbit if s.issubset(host)])\n    rational = Fraction(hit, len(host_orbit))\n    expected_result = dict(M_F=len(small_orbit), M_I_F=inner, M_I=len(host_orbit),\n                           containing=hit, p_num=rational.numerator, p_den=rational.denominator)\n    # Recheck all integers and the cross-multiplied symmetry witness in exact Python.\n    if any(type(v) is not int for v in observed.values()) or observed != expected_result:\n        system.exit(5)\n    if hit * len(small_orbit) != inner * len(host_orbit):\n        system.exit(6)\nsystem.exit(0)\n",
    "output_contract": "One object per case: M_F, M_I_F, M_I, containing, p_num, p_den. Counts are distinct ordinary edge-set copies; the last fields give the reduced exact fixed-F containment probability.",
    "reason": "Finite constructive subroutine for the stated ordinary-copy symmetry identity at eq:copy-ratio. It enumerates exact copy counts and fixed-copy containment probabilities on bounded hosts. It does not implement or certify the general threshold theorem, spreadness, hierarchy selection, posterior resampling or tree reduction.",
    "reference_code": "# INPUTS: JSON list of {n,I,F}; I,F are lists of unordered vertex pairs.\n# PRECONDITIONS: 2 <= n <= 4; nonempty simple I; F subset I; vertices are integers in [0,n).\n# OUTPUTS: JSON list of ordinary-copy counts and exact containment probability numerator/denominator.\n# TIME: O(B*n!*n^2) for B cases; n is bounded above by 4.\n# SPACE: O(B*2^(n*(n-1)/2)*n^2), including cached distinct edge-set copies.\n# ARITHMETIC: Python exact integers and kit python-flint fmpq; no floating-point operations.\n# FAILURE: invalid input raises ValueError and exits nonzero; no threshold certification is attempted.\nimport itertools, json, sys\nfrom functools import lru_cache\nfrom flint import fmpq\n\ndef edges(raw, n):\n    if not isinstance(raw, list):\n        raise ValueError('edge list required')\n    result = []\n    for e in raw:\n        if not isinstance(e, list) or len(e) != 2 or any(type(v) is not int for v in e):\n            raise ValueError('integer pair required')\n        u, v = sorted(e)\n        if not 0 <= u < v < n:\n            raise ValueError('invalid simple edge')\n        result.append((u, v))\n    if len(set(result)) != len(result):\n        raise ValueError('duplicate edge')\n    return frozenset(result)\n\n@lru_cache(None)\ndef copies(n, pattern):\n    vertices = sorted({v for e in pattern for v in e})\n    images = []\n    for placement in itertools.permutations(range(n), len(vertices)):\n        mapping = dict(zip(vertices, placement))\n        images.append(frozenset(tuple(sorted((mapping[u], mapping[v]))) for u, v in pattern))\n    return set(images)\n\ndef solve(case):\n    n = case['n']\n    if type(n) is not int or not 2 <= n <= 4:\n        raise ValueError('n outside finite contract')\n    I, F = edges(case['I'], n), edges(case['F'], n)\n    if not I or not F <= I:\n        raise ValueError('nonempty I and F subset I required')\n    CI, CF = copies(n, I), copies(n, F)\n    contained = sum(F <= image for image in CI)\n    inside = sum(image <= I for image in CF)\n    probability = fmpq(contained, len(CI))\n    return {'M_F': len(CF), 'M_I_F': inside, 'M_I': len(CI),\n            'containing': contained, 'p_num': int(probability.numerator),\n            'p_den': int(probability.denominator)}\n\ndata = json.load(sys.stdin)\nif not isinstance(data, list) or not 1 <= len(data) <= 1000:\n    raise ValueError('bounded nonempty batch required')\nprint(json.dumps([solve(c) for c in data], sort_keys=True))\n",

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Conditional use in software that analyses ordinary pattern occurrence in a binomial random graph. A concrete software integration and its performance guarantees are ABSENT. |
| games_simulation | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Conditional use as a mathematical bound for random-topology simulations matching the source model; game mechanics and empirical simulator validation are ABSENT. |
| tools | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Conditional use as a threshold-bound reporting tool supplied with the correct graph expectation threshold. The paper does not supply a production tool or an efficient expectation-threshold evaluator. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | stated | The source states a deterministic simultaneous tree reduction. It supports adaptation of the mathematical procedure for an explicitly represented finite tree; an efficient implementation and arithmetic cost model are ABSENT. |
| data_structures | True | moderate | adaptable | stated | The probability tree explicitly stores separate extension histories, child distributions and arc labels. This is a source-stated mathematical representation; implementation complexity is ABSENT. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Hardware kernels, parallel execution bounds and accelerator mappings are ABSENT. Finite probability-tree mathematics does not supply such a contract. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Cryptographic primitives, adversary models and computational hardness guarantees are ABSENT. The theorem concerns ordinary graph-copy occurrence under an independent-edge law. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Learning, estimation and generalisation guarantees are ABSENT. Posterior resampling here is a finite mathematical transition, not a validated learning method. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Optimisation solver guarantees, scheduling objectives and approximation contracts are ABSENT. Subgraph maximisation inside the proof does not establish an efficient optimisation method. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Protocol, routing, consensus and service-availability guarantees are ABSENT. Ordinary pattern occurrence in a binomial random graph alone does not certify distributed operation. |
| other (Random graph containment and probabilistic combinatorics) | True | strong | adaptable | stated | Direct mathematical applicability to ordinary subgraph containment in finite binomial random graphs, with targets allowed to vary with the host. This is a probability threshold bound, not an algorithmic cost guarantee. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L-finite-simple** (hypothesis): The main comparison requires a finite simple target with at least one edge, fitting within the finite host; edgeless targets and targets exceeding the host are outside its stated regime. Blocked use: ['Direct application to directed, weighted or multigraph targets.', 'Application to edgeless or oversized targets.']
- **L-ordinary** (hypothesis): Containment means ordinary copies, allowing additional edges among selected vertices. An induced-copy threshold is not established. Blocked use: ['Induced-pattern threshold certification.']
- **L-product** (hypothesis): The graph law has independent edges with a common inclusion probability. Correlated or heterogeneous edge models require a separate justification. Blocked use: ['Transfer to dependent failures or unequal edge probabilities.', 'Deterministic network reliability guarantees.']
- **L-expectation** (hypothesis): The expectation threshold imposes a constraint for every target subgraph, not merely for the full target. Blocked use: ['Replacing the expectation threshold by a full-target first-moment estimate alone.']
- **L-clipping** (edge_case): The upper bound is clipped at the maximum possible density and can therefore give only a trivial threshold bound. It does not identify an exact threshold. Blocked use: ['Treating the upper bound as an equality or as a useful sparse-density estimate in every instance.']
- **L-disjoint** (hypothesis): The tree must be disjoint along each path; labels on different branches may overlap. Arbitrary overlapping histories do not satisfy this hypothesis. Blocked use: ['Reusing the same product sample on a tree with overlapping labels along a path.']
- **L-node-spread** (hypothesis): Spread is required for each individual child law, rather than assumed for the distribution of a full path. Blocked use: ['Substitution of a full-path spread condition without establishing the required nodewise laws.']
- **L-capacities** (hypothesis): Tree covering requires positive integer capacities with the stated geometric separation and positive spread parameter. Blocked use: ['Application to arbitrary capacity profiles or nonpositive spread parameters.']
- **L-resampling** (hypothesis): The local resampling estimate requires the stated spread-to-density ratio and an independent product sample; endpoint densities do not satisfy the local lemma hypotheses. Blocked use: ['Using the local failure estimate outside its density or spread regime.']
- **L-outside-function** (hypothesis): The distortion estimate applies to nonnegative functions of coordinates outside the fixed target label. Blocked use: ['Applying distortion to a function inspecting target-label coordinates or changing sign.']
- **L-target-law** (edge_case): At fixed sample the source retains its original law, while the target may not. The target cannot be assumed to have the original child law before the conditioning analysis. Blocked use: ['Using unconditioned target marginals as the original spread law at fixed sample.']
- **L-isolates** (edge_case): Deleting isolated vertices preserves the containment event under the host-size and ordinary-copy assumptions; the expectation threshold remains defined using the original target. Blocked use: ['Recomputing the expectation threshold on the nonisolated target without justification.']
- **L-copy-normalization** (hypothesis): Copy counts are counts of subgraphs rather than vertex embeddings; the automorphism normalization cannot be omitted. Blocked use: ['Using raw embedding counts in the copy-ratio identity.']
- **L-computation** (unknown): A practical runtime bound for the complete hierarchy and reduction implementation is ABSENT. The finite deterministic construction alone does not establish efficient computation. Blocked use: ['Claiming a polynomial-time threshold calculator or scalable path-search implementation.']
- **L-formal** (formal_scope): An end-to-end formal proof certificate for the full repaired paper is ABSENT from the source inspected for this limits block. Blocked use: ['Treating schema validation or finite enumeration as formal certification of the full containment theorem.']

> ## Scope
>
> The second Kahn–Kalai conjecture compares the threshold for a random graph to contain a fixed graph with its expectation threshold. The formalized result proves the comparison up to a universal factor times $1+\log_2|E(H)|$, and hence up to a universal factor times $\log_2 n$, for every graph $H$ with at least one edge and at most $n$ vertices, $n\ge2$. The bounds use the actual containment and expectation thresholds.

Comparator source `SecondKahnKalai.lean` (2737 bytes, sha256 `2a0d29f9fde90aa8a4b5173da67cab115be047e59f6001ccdc9b6095988beae5`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib.Data.Fintype.Powerset
import Mathlib.Basic.Real.Basic
import Mathlib.Algebra.BigOperators.Ring.Finset
import Mathlib.Combinatorics.SimpleGraph.Copy
import Mathlib.Analysis.SpecialFunctions.Log.Basic

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

universe u

namespace LeanBlast.SecondKahnKalai

def bernoulliWeight {X : Type u} [Fintype X] [DecidableEq X]
    (p : ℝ) (W : Finset X) : ℝ :=
  ∏ x, if x ∈ W then p else 1 - p

def bernoulliExpectation {X : Type u} [Fintype X] [DecidableEq X]
    (p : ℝ) (f : Finset X → ℝ) : ℝ :=
  ∑ W, bernoulliWeight p W * f W

def bernoulliProbability {X : Type u} [Fintype X] [DecidableEq X]
    (p : ℝ) (P : Finset X → Prop) : ℝ := by
  classical
  exact ∑ W, if P W then bernoulliWeight p W else 0

/-- The unordered non-loop edges of the complete graph on `n` vertices. -/
abbrev Edge (n : ℕ) := {e : Sym2 (Fin n) // ¬e.IsDiag}

/-- A set of edges, interpreted as an ordinary graph on all `n` vertices. -/
def graphOfEdges {n : ℕ} (W : Finset (Edge n)) : SimpleGraph (Fin n) :=
  SimpleGraph.fromEdgeSet (Subtype.val '' (W : Set (Edge n)))

/-- The number of unordered edges, each counted once. -/
def edgeCount {V : Type u} (H : SimpleGraph V) : ℕ := Nat.card H.edgeSet

/-- Expected number of actual unlabelled non-induced copies in `G(n,p)`. -/
def expectedCopies {V : Type u} (n : ℕ) (p : ℝ) (H : SimpleGraph V) : ℝ :=
  bernoulliExpectation p (fun W : Finset (Edge n) =>
    ((graphOfEdges W).copyCount H : ℝ))

/-- Probability that `G(n,p)` contains at least one non-induced copy. -/
def containmentProbability {V : Type u} (n : ℕ) (p : ℝ)
    (H : SimpleGraph V) : ℝ :=
  bernoulliProbability p (fun W : Finset (Edge n) =>
    0 < (graphOfEdges W).copyCount H)

def criticalThreshold {V : Type u} (n : ℕ) (H : SimpleGraph V) : ℝ :=
  sInf {p : ℝ | p ∈ Set.Icc 0 1 ∧ (1 / 2 : ℝ) ≤ containmentProbability n p H}

def expectationThreshold {V : Type u} (n : ℕ) (H : SimpleGraph V) : ℝ :=
  sInf {p : ℝ | p ∈ Set.Icc 0 1 ∧
    ∀ F : H.Subgraph, (1 / 2 : ℝ) ≤ expectedCopies n p F.coe}

def logTwo (x : ℝ) : ℝ := Real.log x / Real.log 2

def SecondKahnKalaiBounds {V : Type u} (n : ℕ) (H : SimpleGraph V) : Prop :=
  criticalThreshold n H ≤
      min 1 (2048 * Real.exp 50 * expectationThreshold n H *
        (1 + logTwo (edgeCount H : ℝ))) ∧
    criticalThreshold n H ≤
      6144 * Real.exp 50 * expectationThreshold n H * logTwo (n : ℝ)

theorem secondKahnKalaiBounds (n : ℕ) (V : Type u) [Fintype V]
    (H : SimpleGraph V) (hn : 2 ≤ n) (hcard : Fintype.card V ≤ n)
    (hedge : 1 ≤ edgeCount H) : SecondKahnKalaiBounds n H := by
  sorry

end LeanBlast.SecondKahnKalai

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the second Kahn–Kalai conjecture. For every finite simple graph <i>H</i> with <i>h</i> ≥ 1 edges and at most <i>n</i> vertices, the threshold for $`G(n,p)`$ to contain an ordinary copy of <i>H</i> is at most $`C p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)(1+\log_2 h)`$, where <i>C</i> is universal. Here $`p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)`$ is the least density at which every subgraph of <i>H</i> has expected copy count at least one half.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

| anchor | kind | label | number | first sentence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| [stmt-thm-main](#stmt-thm-main) | theorem | `thm:main` | 1 | For every $n\ge2$ and every finite simple graph $H$ with $h=e(H)\ge1$ edges and at most $n$ vertices, |
| [stmt-lem-local](#stmt-lem-local) | lemma | `lem:local` | 2 | Suppose that $&#124;A_b&#124;\le m$ for a positive integer $m$, the law $\nu$ is $a$-spread, and $$0<\rho<1,\qquad a/\rho\le\mathrm e^{-50}.$$ Let $W\sim\mu_\rho$, independently of the source choice, and perfor |
| [stmt-thm-tree](#stmt-thm-tree) | theorem | `thm:tree` | 3 | Let $\mathcal T$ be a disjoint probability tree on a finite set $X$, with $k\ge1$ levels. |
| [stmt-lem-reduction](#stmt-lem-reduction) | lemma | `lem:reduction` | 4 | Let $\mathcal T$ be a disjoint probability tree with $k\ge1$ levels and positive integer capacities $m_1,\ldots,m_k$ satisfying $m_{j+1}\ge16m_j$. |
| [stmt-lem-hierarchy](#stmt-lem-hierarchy) | lemma | `lem:hierarchy` | 5 | There is a disjoint probability tree on $X=\binom{[n]}2$ whose path unions are copies of $H$ with its isolated vertices removed, such that every level is $\sigma$-spread and its label sizes |

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Graph thresholds and the main result

When does a random graph contain a prescribed graph? A necessary condition is that each subgraph of the target have a reasonable expected number of copies. Kahn and Kalai <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[KK07](#ref-KK07)] conjectured that this elementary obstruction determines the containment threshold within a logarithmic factor, uniformly over the target graph. We prove this conjecture, with the logarithm depending on the number of target edges.

For finite simple graphs $G$ and $F$, write $N(G,F)$ for the number of subgraphs of $G$ isomorphic to $F$. These are ordinary copies: additional edges between the chosen vertices are allowed. Let $G(n,p)$ be the random graph on $[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}$ in which each edge is present independently with probability $p$. For a graph $H$ with at least one edge and at most $n$ vertices, define $$\begin{aligned}
 p_{\mathrm c}(n,H)
 &=\inf\{p\in[0,1]:\mathbb P(N(G(n,p),H)>0)\ge\tfrac12\},\\
 p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)
 &=\inf\{p\in[0,1]:\mathbb EN(G(n,p),F)\ge\tfrac12
                 \text{ for every subgraph }F\subseteq H\}.
\end{aligned}$$ The first quantity is the containment threshold; the second is the *graph expectation threshold*. Both infima are attained, since the relevant probabilities and expectations are continuous, there are only finitely many constraints, and $p=1$ satisfies all of them. The single-edge constraint gives $p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)>0$. If $H$ appears with probability at least one half, then so does every $F\subseteq H$, and its expected count is at least one half. Thus $p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)\le p_{\mathrm c}(n,H)$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:stronger"></a>
**Theorem 1**. *For every $n\ge2$ and every finite simple graph $H$ with $h=e(H)\ge1$ edges and at most $n$ vertices, $$\label{eq:stronger}
 p_{\mathrm c}(n,H)\le
 \min\{1,\;2048\mathrm e^{50}p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)(1+\log_2 h)\}.$$ In particular, $$p_{\mathrm c}(n,H)\le6144\mathrm e^{50}p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)\log_2 n.$$*

</div>

Here $\mathrm e$ is the base of the natural logarithm. The constants are explicit and universal; the graph $H$ may vary with $n$. Kahn and Kalai use expected count one in their formulation. Replacing one half by one changes the graph expectation threshold by a factor between one and two: for each subgraph with $r\ge1$ edges its expected count is proportional to $p^r$, so its constraint changes by $2^{1/r}\le2$. This normalization therefore gives the same conjecture.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### History and the obstruction

For a fixed target, threshold questions are part of the classical random-graph theory of Erdős and Rényi <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[ER60](#ref-ER60)]; Bollobás <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Bollobas81](#ref-Bollobas81)] treated threshold functions for general fixed subgraphs. Here the target can grow with the host graph. For example, let $H$ be a perfect matching on an even number $n$ of vertices. Then $p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)=\Theta(1/n)$, whereas $p_{\mathrm c}(n,H)=\Theta(\log n/n)$ <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[ER66](#ref-ER66), [KK07](#ref-KK07)]. The larger scale is needed to eliminate isolated vertices of the host: a perfect matching must cover every vertex. Hamilton cycles exhibit the same logarithmic gap <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[KK07](#ref-KK07)]. These examples show why a uniform comparison must allow a logarithm. Other spanning targets have required different methods: Riordan <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Riordan00](#ref-Riordan00)], for example, proved that for every fixed $p>1/4$, $G(2^d,p)$ contains the $d$-dimensional cube with probability tending to one.

To distinguish the two Kahn–Kalai conjectures, consider a nonempty proper increasing family $\mathcal F\subseteq2^X$, where $X$ is finite. A *cover* is a family $\mathcal G\subseteq2^X$ such that every $F\in\mathcal F$ contains some $S\in\mathcal G$. Its cost at density $p$ is $\sum_{S\in\mathcal G}p^{|S|}$. The integral expectation threshold $q(\mathcal F)$ is the largest $p$ for which a cover has cost at most $1/2$. A fractional cover assigns weights $w_S\ge0$ to $S\subseteq X$, requires $\sum_{S\subseteq F}w_S\ge1$ for each $F\in\mathcal F$, and has cost $\sum_S w_Sp^{|S|}$. Allowing these covers defines $q_f(\mathcal F)$ with the same budget $1/2$. Union bounds and expectations give $q(\mathcal F)\le q_f(\mathcal F)\le p_{\mathrm c}(\mathcal F)$, where $p_{\mathrm c}(\mathcal F)$ is the density at which an independent random subset belongs to $\mathcal F$ with probability $1/2$.

Talagrand <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Tal10](#ref-Tal10), Section 6] introduced the fractional formulation and its dual description by spread probability measures. Alweiss, Lovett, Wu, and Zhang <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[ALWZ21](#ref-ALWZ21)], building on the regularity viewpoint of Lovett, Solomon, and Zhang <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[LSZ19](#ref-LSZ19)], developed the fragment method in their work on sunflowers. Frankston, Kahn, Narayanan, and Park <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[FKNP21](#ref-FKNP21)] proved $p_{\mathrm c}(\mathcal F)=O(q_f(\mathcal F)\log\ell)$, where $\ell$ is the maximum of two and the largest size of a minimal member of $\mathcal F$. Park and Pham <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[PP24](#ref-PP24)] proved the corresponding bound with $q$ in place of $q_f$, resolving the abstract Kahn–Kalai conjecture by a minimum-fragment argument. The integral and fractional thresholds are within a universal constant factor at the common one-half budget <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[OpenAIThresholdEquivalence2026](#ref-OpenAIThresholdEquivalence2026), Theorem 1.1]. This comparison is contextual; the proof below does not use it.

For the family $\mathcal F_H$ of edge sets containing a copy of $H$, the copies of any one subgraph form a cover. Thus $p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)\le q(\mathcal F_H)$, and the abstract theorem alone does not give the desired bound in terms of $p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)$. Mossel, Niles-Weed, Sun, and Zadik <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[MNSZ22](#ref-MNSZ22), Theorem 1] obtained a single logarithm using the modified graph threshold $$\widetilde p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)
 =\min\{p:\mathbb EN(G(n,p),F)\ge N(H,F)/2
                  \text{ for every }F\subseteq H\}.$$ The factor $N(H,F)$ records that containing $H$ supplies all its copies of $F$. By symmetry this parameter equals $q_f(\mathcal F_H)$ <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[DKP25](#ref-DKP25), Equation (1)]; comparing it with $p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)$ is the additional graph-specific problem.

Dubroff, Kahn, and Park <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[DKP25](#ref-DKP25), Theorems 1.2–1.5] proved $q_f(\mathcal F_H)=O(p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)\log^2 n)$ and hence $p_{\mathrm c}(n,H)=O(p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)\log^3 n)$, using a rooted-extension decomposition. They also established the conjectured single-logarithm bound when $p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)<1/(3n)$. In further work <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[DKP25Cases](#ref-DKP25Cases), Theorems 1.4–1.5], they proved $N(H,F)\le\mathbb EN(G(n,\min\{1,Lq\}),F)$ whenever all subgraphs of $H$ have expected count at least one at density $q$, for test graphs $F$ that are cliques, cycles, or bounded-degree trees. The constant $L$ is universal for cliques and cycles and depends on the degree bound for trees. Tran <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Tran26](#ref-Tran26)] then obtained $q_f(\mathcal F_H)=O(p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)\log(2e(H)))$, reducing the general containment loss to $O(\log^2(2e(H)))$. He removed the loss in the comparison of expectation thresholds for trees and for graphs whose average degree is at least the logarithm of their maximum degree, thereby proving the second conjecture for these classes. His ratio-maximizing cores give the conditional graph-extension distributions used here.

Tran <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Tran26](#ref-Tran26), Section 4] also proposed a coupling conjecture that would absorb successive conditional extensions while paying the logarithmic sampling cost once. We prove the required containment bound through a tree reduction: we keep all possible extension histories in a tree and reduce every level with the same random set. Disjointness along a history supplies the precise control of dependence that this reuse requires.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof strategy and method ancestry

Start with a nonempty edge subset $I$ of $H$. Among its subgraphs, choose $S$ maximizing the probability that a fixed copy $S_0$ on $[n]$ lies in a uniform copy of $I$, after division by a suitable exponential weight in $e(S)$. The first-moment constraints force $S$ to be smaller than $I$ by a fixed factor. Maximality also bounds the probability that a prescribed set $J$ of added edges appears, conditional on any copy of $S$, by $(C_0p_{\mathrm E}(n,H))^{|J|}$ for a universal constant $C_0$. Repeating the choice gives nested subgraphs. The tree branches over all their possible copies; its arc labels are the added edges. Those labels are disjoint along each path, and their sizes increase geometrically. This maximization argument is the graph-specific spread-link construction of Tran <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Tran26](#ref-Tran26), Section 3]. Its conditioning step has antecedents in the large-link argument of Lovett, Solomon, and Zhang <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[LSZ19](#ref-LSZ19), Claim 29] and the spread-link extraction from a maximal violating core in Alweiss, Lovett, Wu, and Zhang <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[ALWZ21](#ref-ALWZ21), Lemma 2.4].

For one distribution of labels, we use the Bayesian resampling construction of Mossel, Niles-Weed, Sun, and Zadik <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[MNSZ25](#ref-MNSZ25)]. Plant a source label into an independent random set $W$, sample a target label from the posterior distribution given their union, and retain the part of the target outside $W$. This remainder is also a subset of the source. Section <a href="#sec:resampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:resampling">2</a> proves that large remainders are exponentially unlikely and bounds the change of measure on coordinates outside the target.

Section <a href="#sec:trees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:trees">3</a> applies this transition simultaneously at every node of the fixed tree. A recursively defined potential bounds the probability that too many continuations fail. Below a target arc, all labels avoid that arc’s label, so the local change-of-measure estimate applies to the entire descendant potential. This is the step that permits one $W$ to halve every level’s capacity while preserving spread at a controlled conditioning cost. Iteration needs only logarithmically many successful draws. Finally, Section <a href="#sec:graphs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:graphs">4</a> constructs the graph hierarchy, with the ordinary-copy normalization explicit, and applies the tree covering theorem. All these arguments are proved below.

Throughout, $\log$ without a subscript is the natural logarithm. For a finite set $X$ and $0\le\rho\le1$, let $\mu_\rho$ be the law of a random subset of $X$ that includes each element independently with probability $\rho$.

<a id="sec:resampling"></a>
### Resampling a spread family

The graph argument will produce distributions of edge extensions. We first show how one product sample can replace a set from such a distribution by a smaller remainder, and bound the resulting change in the law of coordinates outside the chosen set.

For $a>0$, a probability distribution on subsets $A\subseteq X$ is *$a$-spread* if <a id="eq:spread"></a>$$\label{eq:spread}
 \mathbb P(J\subseteq A)\le a^{|J|}
 \qquad(\varnothing\ne J\subseteq X).$$ We allow the distribution to be carried by a finite indexed family $(A_b)_{b\in B}$: distinct indices may have the same label, and labels may be empty. Write $\nu_b>0$ for the weights, with $\sum_b\nu_b=1$.

The transition below is the planted-set and posterior-resampling construction of Mossel, Niles-Weed, Sun, and Zadik <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[MNSZ25](#ref-MNSZ25), Sections 2.1–2.3]. We record a capacity-dependent failure estimate and a change-of-measure bound for functions of coordinates outside the target label; the latter will control the dependence between successive tree levels.

Fix $0<\rho<1$ and $W\subseteq X$. The following transition will be used at every node of a tree:

1.  Choose a *source* $c$ with law $\nu$, and set $Y=W\cup A_c$.

<a id="eq:posterior"></a>
2.  Choose a *target* $b$ with conditional probabilities $$\label{eq:posterior}
      \frac{\nu_b\rho^{-|A_b|}\mathbf 1_{\{A_b\subseteq Y\}}}{Z(Y)},
      \qquad
      Z(Y)=\sum_{d\in B}\nu_d\rho^{-|A_d|}
                      \mathbf 1_{\{A_d\subseteq Y\}}.$$

3.  Retain the fragment $T=A_b\setminus W$.

The denominator is positive because $A_c\subseteq Y$ and $\nu_c>0$. To see why these are posterior probabilities, now let $W\sim\mu_\rho$ independently of the source. For a fixed source $c$, the probability mass of the planted union at a set $Y$ is $\mu_\rho(Y)\rho^{-|A_c|}\mathbf 1_{\{A_c\subseteq Y\}}$. After averaging over $c$, the law of $Y$ therefore has density $Z$ relative to $\mu_\rho$, and Bayes’ formula gives <a href="#eq:posterior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:posterior">[eq:posterior]</a>. At a specified $W$, however, only the source is guaranteed to have its original law $\nu$; the target law may differ.

<a id="eq:two-inclusions"></a>
For every transition of positive probability, $$\label{eq:two-inclusions}
 T\subseteq A_c\cap A_b,
 \qquad A_b\subseteq W\cup T.$$ The first inclusion will preserve spread by comparing the fragment with the source. The second lets us recover the chosen target from $W$ and its fragment. In the tree application, that target also specifies which child and its continuations are kept. The entire transition, including its fragment, only inspects membership in $W$ on $\bigcup_{b\in B}A_b$.

<a id="stmt-lem-local"></a>
<div id="lem:local" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:local-failure"></a>
<a id="eq:distortion"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Local resampling). *Suppose that $|A_b|\le m$ for a positive integer $m$, the law $\nu$ is $a$-spread, and $$0<\rho<1,\qquad a/\rho\le\mathrm e^{-50}.$$ Let $W\sim\mu_\rho$, independently of the source choice, and perform the transition above. Call the transition a *local failure* if $$Z(Y)<\mathrm e^{-10m}\quad\text{or}\quad |T|>m/2.$$ Then $$\label{eq:local-failure}
 \mathbb P(\text{local failure})\le\mathrm e^{-9m}.$$ Moreover, for each fixed target $b$ and every nonnegative function $f$ depending only on $W\cap(X\setminus A_b)$, $$\label{eq:distortion}
 \mathbb E\!\left[
  \mathbf 1_{\{\mathrm{target}=b,\ Z(Y)\ge\mathrm e^{-10m}\}} f(W)
 \right]
 \le \mathrm e^{11m}\nu_b\,\mathbb Ef(W).$$ The expectation on the right is with respect to $W\sim\mu_\rho$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-local"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-local</summary>

<a id="eq:small-denominator"></a>
*Proof.* The density identity above gives $$\label{eq:small-denominator}
 \mathbb P\bigl(Z(Y)<\mathrm e^{-10m}\bigr)
 =\mathbb E_{Y\sim\mu_\rho}
   \bigl[Z(Y)\mathbf 1_{\{Z(Y)<\mathrm e^{-10m}\}}\bigr]
 \le\mathrm e^{-10m}.$$

<a id="eq:intersection"></a>
We will use the following consequence of spread. For a fixed $b$, let $R=|A_c\cap A_b|$, where $c$ has law $\nu$. For every $0\le r\le m$, including the trivial case $r=0$, $$\label{eq:intersection}
 \mathbb P(R=r)
 \le\sum_{\substack{J\subseteq A_b\\|J|=r}}
        \mathbb P(J\subseteq A_c)
 \le\binom mr a^r.$$ On $Z(Y)\ge\mathrm e^{-10m}$, bound the reciprocal denominator in <a href="#eq:posterior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:posterior">[eq:posterior]</a> by $\mathrm e^{10m}$. For a fixed pair $(c,b)$, the condition $A_b\subseteq W\cup A_c$ requires $A_b\setminus A_c\subseteq W$, an event of probability $\rho^{|A_b\setminus A_c|}$. Also, $|T|>m/2$ implies $|A_c\cap A_b|>m/2$. Therefore $$\begin{aligned}
 &\mathbb P\bigl(Z(Y)\ge\mathrm e^{-10m},\ |T|>m/2\bigr)\\
 &\qquad\le
 \mathrm e^{10m}\sum_{c,b}\nu_c\nu_b
 \rho^{-|A_c\cap A_b|}
 \mathbf 1_{\{|A_c\cap A_b|>m/2\}}\\
 &\qquad\le
 \mathrm e^{10m}\sum_{r>m/2}\binom mr(a/\rho)^r
 \le \mathrm e^{10m}2^m\mathrm e^{-25m}.
\end{aligned}$$ Together with <a href="#eq:small-denominator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-denominator">[eq:small-denominator]</a>, this gives $$\mathbb P(\text{local failure})
 \le\mathrm e^{-10m}+\mathrm e^{-(15-\log 2)m}
 \le\mathrm e^{-9m},$$ as $m\ge1$.

For <a href="#eq:distortion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distortion">[eq:distortion]</a>, again bound the reciprocal denominator and then sum over sources. The event $A_b\setminus A_c\subseteq W$ uses only coordinates inside $A_b$, so it is independent of $f(W)$. Hence $$\begin{aligned}
 &\mathbb E\!\left[
 \mathbf 1_{\{\mathrm{target}=b,\ Z(Y)\ge\mathrm e^{-10m}\}} f(W)
 \right]\\
 &\qquad\le
 \mathrm e^{10m}\nu_b\sum_c\nu_c\rho^{-|A_b|}
       \mathbb E\bigl[\mathbf 1_{\{A_b\setminus A_c\subseteq W\}}f(W)\bigr]\\
 &\qquad=
 \mathrm e^{10m}\nu_b\,\mathbb Ef(W)
       \sum_c\nu_c\rho^{-|A_c\cap A_b|}\\
 &\qquad\le
 \mathrm e^{10m}\nu_b\,\mathbb Ef(W)
       \sum_{r=0}^m\binom mr(a/\rho)^r
 \le\mathrm e^{11m}\nu_b\,\mathbb Ef(W).
\end{aligned}$$ The penultimate inequality is <a href="#eq:intersection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intersection">[eq:intersection]</a>; the last uses $(1+a/\rho)^m\le\mathrm e^m$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:trees"></a>
### Covering a tree

We now cover an entire chain of extensions. The input is a finite tree whose edge labels are disjoint along each path and whose child laws are spread. The aim is to put the labels of one full path inside a product sample, with a cost logarithmic in the largest extension size.

A *probability tree on $X$* is a finite rooted tree with all leaves at the same depth, together with a probability distribution $\nu_v$ on the children $\operatorname{Ch}(v)$ of each non-leaf node $v$ and a label $A_v(b)\subseteq X$ on the arc from $v$ to each child $b$. We delete zero-weight children. Different nodes or arcs may carry identical labels; nodes are never identified merely because their labels agree. A *path* always means a full root-to-leaf path, unless otherwise specified. Write $$\mathcal U(P)=\bigcup_{(v,b)\in P}A_v(b)$$ for its union of labels.

The tree is *disjoint* if the labels along each path are pairwise disjoint. A level has *capacity* $m$ if all its labels have size at most $m$. A level is *$a$-spread* if the child distribution at every node immediately above that level is $a$-spread. Thus all requirements concern individual child distributions; no spread assumption is made on the distribution of a full path.

<a id="stmt-thm-tree"></a>
<div id="thm:tree" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3** (Tree covering). *Let $\mathcal T$ be a disjoint probability tree on a finite set $X$, with $k\ge1$ levels. Suppose that every level is $\sigma$-spread, where $\sigma>0$, and that its positive integer capacities satisfy $$\ell_{i+1}\ge16\ell_i\qquad(1\le i<k).$$ Set $$r=\min\{1,\;16\mathrm e^{50}\sigma(1+\log_2\ell_k)\}.$$ Then $$\mathbb P_{W\sim\mu_r}
 \bigl(\mathcal U(P)\subseteq W\text{ for some path }P\text{ of }\mathcal T\bigr)
 \ge\tfrac23.$$*

</div>

Section <a href="#sec:graphs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:graphs">4</a> constructs such a tree for $H$ with $\sigma=128p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)$ and $\ell_k\le e(H)$. We prove the tree theorem by repeatedly pruning and reweighting the tree while replacing its labels by smaller subsets. Empty labels and unequal label sizes must therefore be allowed.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### One simultaneous reduction

<a id="stmt-lem-reduction"></a>
<div id="lem:reduction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Reduction). *Let $\mathcal T$ be a disjoint probability tree with $k\ge1$ levels and positive integer capacities $m_1,\ldots,m_k$ satisfying $m_{j+1}\ge16m_j$. Suppose level $j$ is $\alpha_j$-spread, where $$0<\alpha_j\le a,\qquad \rho=\mathrm e^{50}a<1.$$ There is a deterministic procedure which, given $W\sim\mu_\rho$, succeeds with probability at least $3/4$ and on success returns a disjoint probability tree $\mathcal T'$ with the same levels, such that:*

1.  *level $j$ has capacity $\lfloor m_j/2\rfloor$ and is $\alpha_j/(1-\mathrm e^{-m_j})$-spread;*

<a id="eq:recovery"></a>
2.  *for every path $Q$ of $\mathcal T'$ there is a path $P$ of $\mathcal T$ with $$\label{eq:recovery}
       \mathcal U(P)\subseteq W\cup\mathcal U(Q).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-reduction</summary>

<a id="eq:local-masses"></a>
*Proof.* The output will be a subtree with labels $A_v(b)\setminus W$ and child laws obtained from the target marginals of the local transition. We first identify which children can be retained while discarding at most $\mathrm e^{-m_j}$ of the transition mass at each retained node with outgoing level $j$. Keep the input tree fixed throughout this analysis. At a node whose outgoing level is $j$, use the transition of Section <a href="#sec:resampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:resampling">2</a> with its child distribution and capacity $m_j$. All nodes use the same set $W$. At fixed $W$, write $d_v(W)$ for the probability of local failure at $v$, and $t_{vb}(W)$ for the probability of local non-failure with target $b$. Thus $$\label{eq:local-masses}
 d_v(W)+\sum_{b\in\operatorname{Ch}(v)}t_{vb}(W)=1.$$ All these probabilities refer to the child laws of the fixed input tree, before any pruning or conditioning.

<a id="eq:good-node"></a>
*Pruning.* For the remainder of the construction, fix $W$ and put $\epsilon_j=\mathrm e^{-m_j}$. Declare all leaves good. Working upward, declare a node $v$ with outgoing level $s$ good if $$\label{eq:good-node}
 \sum_{\substack{b\in\operatorname{Ch}(v)\\b\ \mathrm{good}}}t_{vb}(W)
 \ge1-\epsilon_s.$$ The attempt succeeds exactly when the root is good.

<a id="eq:pruning-recurrence"></a>
To bound the probability that the root is bad, define a nonnegative potential on every node of the input tree. Set $D_v(W)=0$ at leaves and, at a node with outgoing level $s$, define recursively $$\label{eq:pruning-recurrence}
 D_v(W)=\frac{d_v(W)+\sum_{b\in\operatorname{Ch}(v)}t_{vb}(W)D_b(W)}{\epsilon_s}.$$ Every bad node satisfies $D_v(W)>1$. Indeed, assuming this for its bad children, the numerator in <a href="#eq:pruning-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pruning-recurrence">[eq:pruning-recurrence]</a> is at least $$d_v(W)+\sum_{b\ \mathrm{bad}}t_{vb}(W)>\epsilon_s,$$ where the last inequality follows from <a href="#eq:local-masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-masses">[eq:local-masses]</a> and the failure of <a href="#eq:good-node" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-node">[eq:good-node]</a>. This proves the assertion by backward induction.

*Expected potential.* We now average over $W\sim\mu_\rho$. Fix a child $b$ of a node $v$ with outgoing level $s$, and abbreviate its incoming label to $A_b$. Every label in the entire subtree below $b$ is disjoint from $A_b$: each such label lies on a path containing the arc into $b$. The transition at any node in that subtree only inspects $W$ on the union of that node’s child labels. By its recursive definition, $D_b(W)$ therefore depends only on $W$ outside $A_b$.

This statement concerns every node of the fixed input tree, before conditioning on that node being good or being retained. Figure <a href="#fig:outside" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:outside">1</a> shows the disjointness that makes this possible.

<figure id="fig:outside">

<figcaption>Disjointness along paths controls the change of measure. Every arc below $b$ lies on a path through its incoming arc, so the potential $D_{b}$ is a function of coordinates outside the incoming label. The diagram is schematic; labels on different branches may overlap.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-outside"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:outside`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,>=Stealth,
  vertex/.style={circle,draw,fill=white,inner sep=2pt},
  every node/.style={font=\small}]
\node[vertex] (v) at (0,0) {$v$};
\node[vertex] (b) at (-1.8,-1.0) {$b$};
\node[vertex] (c) at (1.8,-1.0) {$c$};
\node[vertex] (b1) at (-2.6,-2.0) {$b_1$};
\node[vertex] (b2) at (-1.0,-2.0) {$b_2$};
\draw[->,thick,blue!65!black] (v)--node[above left] {$A_v(b)$} (b);
\draw[->] (v)--node[above right] {$A_v(c)$} (c);
\draw[->] (b)--(b1);
\draw[->] (b)--(b2);
\draw[densely dotted] (b1)--(-2.9,-2.6);
\draw[densely dotted] (b1)--(-2.3,-2.6);
\draw[densely dotted] (b2)--(-1.3,-2.6);
\draw[densely dotted] (b2)--(-0.7,-2.6);
\node[align=left,anchor=west] at (0.2,-2.0)
  {Every label below $b$ avoids $A_v(b)$.\\
   Hence $D_b(W)$ uses only $W\setminus A_v(b)$.};
\node[anchor=south] at (0,0.4) {The same set $W$ is used at every node.};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 7 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A tree diagram illustrating disjointness along paths:
- Text at the top: "The same set $W$ is used at every node."
- Root node $v$ connects via directed edges to children $b$ and $c$.
- The directed edge from $v$ to $b$ is highlighted in blue with label $A_v(b)$.
- The edge from $v$ to $c$ is labeled $A_v(c)$, and node $c$ has horizontal line shading.
- From node $b$, two branching edges lead down to children labeled $b_1$ and $b_2$.
- Annotations to the right of the $b$-subtree state: "Every label below $b$ avoids $A_v(b)$." and "Hence $D_b(W)$ uses only $W \setminus A_v(b)$."
```


In the local transition at $v$, $$t_{vb}(W)\le
 \mathbb P\bigl(\mathrm{target}=b,\ Z(Y)\ge\mathrm e^{-10m_s}\mid W\bigr).$$ We may consequently apply <a href="#eq:distortion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distortion">[eq:distortion]</a> with $f(W)=D_b(W)$. Together with <a href="#eq:local-failure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-failure">[eq:local-failure]</a> and $\epsilon_s^{-1}=\mathrm e^{m_s}$, the recurrence gives <a id="eq:expected-potential"></a>$$\label{eq:expected-potential}
 \mathbb ED_v(W)
 \le \mathrm e^{-8m_s}
    +\mathrm e^{12m_s}\sum_{b\in\operatorname{Ch}(v)}\nu_v(b)\,\mathbb ED_b(W).$$ This step controls the dependence caused by reusing $W$. Since the child weights sum to one, backward induction yields $$\mathbb ED_v(W)\le
 \sum_{j=s}^k
 \exp\left(12\sum_{h=s}^{j-1}m_h-8m_j\right).$$ In particular, if $o$ is the root, then $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P(o\text{ is bad})
 &\le\mathbb ED_o(W)\\
 &\le\sum_{j=1}^k
   \exp\left(12\sum_{h<j}m_h-8m_j\right)
 <\tfrac14.
\end{aligned}$$ For the last inequality, geometric growth gives $\sum_{h<j}m_h\le m_j/15$, and the $m_j$ are distinct positive integers. Thus the sum is at most $\sum_{m\ge1}\mathrm e^{-7m}<1/4$.

*The reduced tree.* On success, start from the good root. At any good node $v$ with outgoing level $j$, put $$\gamma_v(W)=\sum_{b\ \mathrm{good}}t_{vb}(W)\ge1-\epsilon_j.$$ Retain each good child $b$ with $t_{vb}(W)>0$, give it weight $t_{vb}(W)/\gamma_v(W)$, replace its incoming label by $$T_b=A_v(b)\setminus W,$$ and continue recursively into $b$. The result is a pruned and reweighted subtree of the input tree. It has the same depth because $\gamma_v>0$ at every retained non-leaf node. Its labels are subsets of the old labels, so it is disjoint. Since $A_v(b)\subseteq W\cup T_b$ on every retained arc, it also satisfies <a href="#eq:recovery" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:recovery">[eq:recovery]</a>. A retained child has $t_{vb}>0$, which implies $|T_b|\le\lfloor m_j/2\rfloor$.

This new child law is the target marginal of the joint transition conditioned on local non-failure and a good target. Denote this conditioning event by $E_v$. For the spread bound, use the source: <a href="#eq:two-inclusions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-inclusions">[eq:two-inclusions]</a> gives $T\subseteq A_v(c)$ for every transition, and the unconditioned source has law $\nu_v$ even at fixed $W$. Thus, for every nonempty $J\subseteq X$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P(J\subseteq T\mid E_v,W)
 &\le\frac{\mathbb P_{c\sim\nu_v}(J\subseteq A_v(c))}{\gamma_v(W)}\\
 &\le\frac{\alpha_j^{|J|}}{1-\epsilon_j}
 \le\left(\frac{\alpha_j}{1-\epsilon_j}\right)^{|J|}.
\end{aligned}$$ This proves all the assertions. The quantities $t_{vb}(W)$ define the entire construction deterministically; sources and targets are auxiliary random variables used to analyze its weights. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Iteration

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-reduction-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-reduction</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:tree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tree">3</a>.* If $r=1$, the conclusion is immediate. Suppose $r<1$, and set $$a=4\sigma,\qquad \rho=\mathrm e^{50}a,
 \qquad s=\lfloor\log_2\ell_k\rfloor+1.$$ In particular $0<\rho<1$. Start with $\mathcal T$ and make successive attempts using fresh independent sets of law $\mu_\rho$. A failed attempt leaves the current tree unchanged. After a successful attempt, retain the tree provided by Lemma <a href="#lem:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reduction">4</a>. Conditionally on the complete past, the current tree is fixed and the next draw still has law $\mu_\rho$.

<a id="eq:capacities"></a>
After $t$ successes, the capacity at original level $i$ is $$\label{eq:capacities}
 m_i(t)=\left\lfloor\frac{\ell_i}{2^t}\right\rfloor,$$ using $\lfloor\lfloor x\rfloor/2\rfloor=\lfloor x/2\rfloor$. Levels of zero capacity form an initial segment. After each success, choose any one path through that segment and keep its ending subtree, deleting the segment. All committed labels are empty, so each path of the retained subtree extends to a full path by adjoining the chosen empty prefix. This preserves the recovery guarantee and every bound at a retained node. If no levels remain, only the root is left. Fix a tie-breaking rule for these choices. The remaining positive capacities still satisfy $m_{i+1}(t)\ge16m_i(t)$, since $\lfloor16x\rfloor\ge16\lfloor x\rfloor$.

At a remaining original level $i$, a valid spread parameter after $t$ successes is <a id="eq:spread-iteration"></a>$$\label{eq:spread-iteration}
 \alpha_i(t)=\sigma
 \prod_{u=0}^{t-1}
 \left(1-\mathrm e^{-\lfloor\ell_i/2^u\rfloor}\right)^{-1}.$$ The positive integers $\lfloor\ell_i/2^u\rfloor$ in this product are distinct. The infinite product over all positive integers is less than $4$, because $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{m=1}^{\infty}-\log(1-\mathrm e^{-m})
 &\le \frac{1}{1-\mathrm e^{-1}}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathrm e^{-m}\\
 &=\frac{\mathrm e^{-1}}{(1-\mathrm e^{-1})^2}<\log 4.
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently $\alpha_i(t)\le4\sigma=a$. The initial tree satisfies these bounds, and Lemma <a href="#lem:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reduction">4</a> proves them inductively after every success. Thus every attempt before completion has conditional success probability at least $3/4$.

After $s$ successes, <a href="#eq:capacities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:capacities">[eq:capacities]</a> is zero at every level. Composing <a href="#eq:recovery" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:recovery">[eq:recovery]</a> through the successful reductions shows that the union of their draws contains the labels of a path in the original tree. The expected waiting time for each next success is at most $4/3$: conditionally on the past, its tail is bounded by that of a geometric random variable with success probability $3/4$. Hence the expected number of attempts needed is at most $4s/3$. By Markov’s inequality, completion occurs within $4s$ attempts with probability at least $2/3$.

Predraw $4s$ independent sets of law $\mu_\rho$, including any that the procedure ultimately does not use, and take their union $W_*$. It has law $\mu_{r_*}$, where $$r_*=1-(1-\rho)^{4s}
 \le4s\rho
 =16\mathrm e^{50}\sigma s
 \le16\mathrm e^{50}\sigma(1+\log_2\ell_k)=r.$$ With probability at least $2/3$, $W_*$ contains a path union. The same bound holds for $\mu_r$ by monotonicity. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:graphs"></a>
### Application to graph containment

It remains to construct the tree required by Theorem <a href="#thm:tree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tree">3</a>. We use the normalized-containment maximization and conditional-extension argument of Tran <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Tran26](#ref-Tran26), Section 3, Lemma 3.1], with constants suited to the factor-$16$ separation of capacities. The copy-count identity below also appears in Mossel, Niles-Weed, Sun, and Zadik <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[MNSZ22](#ref-MNSZ22), Equation (5)].

Put $q=p_{\mathrm E}(n,H)$ and $\sigma=128q$. Delete the isolated vertices of $H$ and of each graph subsequently considered, and identify these graphs with their edge sets. This does not change the event of containing $H$: any copy of its nonisolated part can be extended by choosing the required number of additional vertices, since $n\ge|V(H)|$ and copies need not be induced. Every edge subset used below, on its incident vertices, is a subgraph of the original $H$, so its expectation at $q$ is at least $1/2$. Throughout this deletion, $q$ remains the threshold defined using the original graph $H$.

<a id="eq:copy-ratio"></a>
For a graph $F$ without isolated vertices, let $$M(F)=N(K_n,F),\qquad M(I,F)=N(I,F).$$ In particular, $M(F)=(n)_{v(F)}/|\operatorname{Aut}(F)|$, where $(n)_v=n(n-1)\cdots(n-v+1)$ and $\operatorname{Aut}(F)$ is the group of vertex permutations preserving adjacency. Dividing by this group counts each ordinary copy once; $M(I,F)$ likewise counts copies, rather than vertex embeddings. For the empty edge set use $M(\varnothing)=M(I,\varnothing)=1$. If $\boldsymbol I$ is a uniformly chosen copy of $I$ on $[n]$, and $F_0$ is any fixed copy of an edge subset $F\subseteq I$, then $$\label{eq:copy-ratio}
 \mathbb P(F_0\subseteq\boldsymbol I)=\frac{M(I,F)}{M(F)}.$$ Indeed, permutation symmetry makes the probability independent of the chosen copy $F_0$. Summing it over all $M(F)$ copies counts the $M(I,F)$ copies contained in each $\boldsymbol I$.

<a id="stmt-lem-hierarchy"></a>
<div id="lem:hierarchy" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Graph hierarchy). *There is a disjoint probability tree on $X=\binom{[n]}2$ whose path unions are copies of $H$ with its isolated vertices removed, such that every level is $\sigma$-spread and its label sizes $\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_k$ satisfy $$1\le\ell_1,\qquad \ell_{i+1}\ge16\ell_i,
 \qquad \ell_k\le e(H).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hierarchy</summary>

<a id="eq:maximizer"></a>
*Proof.* For a current nonempty edge set $I\subseteq H$, choose an edge subset $S\subseteq I$ maximizing $$\label{eq:maximizer}
 R_\sigma(I,S)=\sigma^{-|S|}\mathbb P(S_0\subseteq\boldsymbol I),$$ where $S_0$ is any fixed copy of $S$ on $[n]$. The empty set is allowed and has value $1$. Fix any deterministic rule for breaking ties.

Write $h_I=|I|$ and $r=|S|>0$. The expectation constraint gives $M(S)q^r\ge1/2$, while $M(I,S)\le\binom{h_I}{r}$. By <a href="#eq:copy-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:copy-ratio">[eq:copy-ratio]</a>, $$R_\sigma(I,S)\le2\binom{h_I}{r}(q/\sigma)^r
           =2\binom{h_I}{r}128^{-r}.$$ For $r\ge h_I/17$, the bound $\binom{h_I}{r}\le(\mathrm eh_I/r)^r$ gives $$R_\sigma(I,S)\le2(17\mathrm e/128)^r<1.$$ Thus every maximizing predecessor satisfies $|S|<|I|/17$.

Repeat the selection until the empty set is reached, and write the resulting nested sequence in reverse order as $$\varnothing=H_0\subset H_1\subset\cdots\subset H_k=H,
 \qquad |H_{i-1}|<|H_i|/17.$$ Here and below $H$ denotes its edge set. Build a tree rooted at the empty set. From each reached copy $S_0$ of $H_{i-1}$, take as children all copies of $H_i$ containing $S_0$, with the uniform distribution, and label the arc to a child $I_0$ by $I_0\setminus S_0$. Such children exist, since every copy of $H_{i-1}$ extends to a copy of $H_i$ on $[n]$. Keep distinct histories as distinct tree nodes. The labels on every path are disjoint and their union is a copy of $H$. Their sizes are $$\ell_i=|H_i|-|H_{i-1}|>0,
 \qquad
 \ell_{i+1}>16|H_i|\ge16\ell_i.$$

<a id="eq:conditional-ratio"></a>
It remains to check conditional spread. At a node $S_0$, let $A$ be its random added label. If $J\cap S_0\ne\varnothing$, then $\mathbb P(J\subseteq A)=0$. Otherwise, for a uniform copy $\boldsymbol I$ of $H_i$, $$\label{eq:conditional-ratio}
 \mathbb P(J\subseteq A)=
 \frac{\mathbb P(S_0\cup J\subseteq\boldsymbol I)}
      {\mathbb P(S_0\subseteq\boldsymbol I)}.$$ If the numerator is positive, $S_0\cup J$ is isomorphic to an edge subset $U\subseteq H_i$. Maximality of $H_{i-1}$ in <a href="#eq:maximizer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:maximizer">[eq:maximizer]</a> gives $$\sigma^{-(|S_0|+|J|)}\mathbb P(S_0\cup J\subseteq\boldsymbol I)
 \le \sigma^{-|S_0|}\mathbb P(S_0\subseteq\boldsymbol I).$$ Together with <a href="#eq:conditional-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conditional-ratio">[eq:conditional-ratio]</a>, this gives $\mathbb P(J\subseteq A)\le\sigma^{|J|}$. If the numerator is zero, the same bound is immediate. This proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hierarchy-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hierarchy</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:tree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tree">3</a> to the tree of Lemma <a href="#lem:hierarchy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hierarchy">5</a>, with $\sigma=128q$. Since $\ell_k\le h=e(H)$, a Bernoulli edge set with parameter $$r=\min\{1,\;2048\mathrm e^{50}q(1+\log_2 h)\}$$ contains a copy of $H$ with probability at least $2/3$. This proves <a href="#eq:stronger" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stronger">[eq:stronger]</a>. Finally, $h\le\binom n2\le n^2$ and $n\ge2$ give $1+\log_2 h\le3\log_2 n$, and hence the stated constant $C=6144\mathrm e^{50}$. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Disjointness along paths controls the change of measure. Every arc below $b$ lies on a path through its incoming arc, so the potential $D_{b}$ is a function of coordinates outside the incoming label. The diagram is schematic; labels on different branches may overlap.",
      "figure_id": "fig:outside",
      "pdf_page": 7,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:outside",
          "exact_quote": "Disjointness along paths controls the change of measure. Every\narc below $b$ lies on a path through its incoming arc, so the potential\n$D_b$ is a function of coordinates outside the incoming label. The diagram\nis schematic; labels on differ",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,>=Stealth,\n  vertex/.style={circle,draw,fill=white,inner sep=2pt},\n  every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n\\node[vertex] (v) at (0,0) {$v$};\n\\node[vertex] (b) at (-1.8,-1.0) {$b$};\n\\node[vertex] (c) at (1.8,-1.0) {$c$};\n\\node[vertex] (b1) at (-2.6,-2.0) {$b_1$};\n\\node[vertex] (b2) at (-1.0,-2.0) {$b_2$};\n\\draw[->,thick,blue!65!black] (v)--node[above left] {$A_v(b)$} (b);\n\\draw[->] (v)--node[above right] {$A_v(c)$} (c);\n\\draw[->] (b)--(b1);\n\\draw[->] (b)--(b2);\n\\draw[densely dotted] (b1)--(-2.9,-2.6);\n\\draw[densely dotted] (b1)--(-2.3,-2.6);\n\\draw[densely dotted] (b2)--(-1.3,-2.6);\n\\draw[densely dotted] (b2)--(-0.7,-2.6);\n\\node[align=left,anchor=west] at (0.2,-2.0)\n  {Every label below $b$ avoids $A_v(b)$.\\\\\n   Hence $D_b(W)$ uses only $W\\setminus A_v(b)$.};\n\\node[anchor=south] at (0,0.4) {The same set $W$ is used at every node.};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A tree diagram illustrating disjointness along paths:\n- Text at the top: \"The same set $W$ is used at every node.\"\n- Root node $v$ connects via directed edges to children $b$ and $c$.\n- The directed edge from $v$ to $b$ is highlighted in blue with label $A_v(b)$.\n- The edge from $v$ to $c$ is labeled $A_v(c)$, and node $c$ has horizontal line shading.\n- From node $b$, two branching edges lead down to children labeled $b_1$ and $b_2$.\n- Annotations to the right of the $b$-subtree state: \"Every label below $b$ avoids $A_v(b)$.\" and \"Hence $D_b(W)$ uses only $W \\setminus A_v(b)$.\""
    }
